I don’t think I would, at least not that same kind of anxious feeling. It’s like the anxiety doesn’t just appear out of nowhere for me — it comes right after the thought, or maybe with the thought.

Like, I can be doing payroll or taxes and I’ll know the numbers match. I mean, I’ll actually see it. I’ll check the totals, and it lines up, and part of me knows it’s okay. But then this thought jumps in anyway, like, “What if there’s one mistake you didn’t notice?” Not even a big obvious one, but some hidden mistake that I somehow missed.

And then it goes from that to, “What if somebody’s check doesn’t go through?” or “What if the firm gets in trouble because of this?” And then I try to push it away, because I know I already checked it. I’ll tell myself, “You saw it, it matched, you don’t need to go back.” But it comes right back, and then I feel like I can’t trust my own eyes, like what I just saw doesn’t count anymore.

So without that kind of thought forcing its way in, I don’t think I’d be anxious in that same way. Maybe normal stress, sure, but not that pressure where I feel like I have to keep going back and checking.

---------- turn 15 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… I’m having a really hard time stopping myself from checking my work. I work with spreadsheets and calculations, and I’ll go back over the same numbers again and again, even after I’ve already checked them. Like I’ll tell myself, “Okay, that’s done,” but then this feeling comes up that I need to look one more time, just to be sure.\n\nAnd it’s not just being careful. I mean, I know my job needs accuracy, but this feels different. I’ll stay at work for hours after everyone else has left, going through the same cells and totals and formulas, and sometimes I’m not even finding anything new. It’s just that if I don’t check it again, I get this awful feeling that something bad is going to happen and it’ll be my fault somehow. So I keep going back to it, even when I’m exhausted and I know I already checked it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, aside from the need to recheck your work?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s like there’s this thought that keeps forcing its way in, even after I’ve already seen that the numbers match. I’ll be looking at payroll or tax-related entries, and I know I checked the totals, I know the formulas looked right, but then my mind says, what if there’s one mistake you didn’t see? Like some hidden error, some tiny thing I missed, and then someone’s paycheck won’t go through, or the firm will get in trouble, and it’ll be because I didn’t catch it.\n\nAnd I try to tell myself, “No, I literally just checked that,” but it doesn’t stick. It’s like I can’t trust what I just saw with my own eyes. The thought comes right back, almost immediately sometimes, like, “Are you sure? Are you really sure?” And then I start feeling like if I ignore it, I’m being careless or irresponsible, even when part of me knows I’m not. It just keeps repeating until I go back and look again.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and behaviors affecting your relationships with family, friends, or colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yes… it is, more than I wanted to admit at first. At work it’s gotten really bad. I actually missed a filing deadline because I couldn’t stop going back over everything, and then I got called into a formal meeting about it. That was horrible, because I was trying so hard to make sure nothing was wrong, and then the checking itself made me miss the actual deadline.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’m just not showing up for things anymore. I used to go to choir rehearsals, and I had commitments at the youth center, and lately I keep missing them because I can’t get out of the office on time. I’ll think I’m leaving at a normal hour, and then I tell myself I just need to check one more section, and suddenly it’s too late. So people are depending on me and I’m not there, and I feel really guilty and embarrassed about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed with anxiety in situations that have nothing to do with your work or checking things?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not completely sure. I don’t think I feel that same kind of anxiety in random situations, if that’s what you mean. Like, I don’t walk around feeling overwhelmed all the time for no reason.\n\nIt’s more that I get overwhelmed when something feels like it’s on me to get right, or when I start worrying I might have missed something. Outside of that… I mean, I can feel stressed like anyone does, but I haven’t really noticed the same intense feeling unless it’s tied to checking or being responsible for something. I guess I’m not sure if I’m answering that the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about work or checking things?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I usually feel anxious if I’m just… not thinking about work or not in that checking mode. Like if I’m just watching something, or talking to someone, or doing something ordinary, I’m not necessarily panicking or feeling that same pressure.\n\nBut I guess it’s hard to separate sometimes, because work kind of follows me in my head. So I might be technically not at work, but then if the thought pops up again, it’s like I’m back in it. If it truly has nothing to do with checking or whether I missed something, then… no, I don’t think the anxiety is there in the same way. At least I haven’t noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious when you are not thinking about work or checking things and there is no apparent reason for the anxiety?\n\nPatient: I… don’t think so, not really. I’m trying to think of a time when I felt anxious and there wasn’t some reason attached to it, and I can’t really come up with one. It’s usually connected to something in my head, like worrying I forgot something or that I need to go back and make sure.\n\nIf I’m genuinely not thinking about work or checking or whether I made a mistake, then I don’t usually feel that same anxiety just out of nowhere. At least, I haven’t noticed that happening. I might feel tense sometimes, but when I stop and look at it, there’s usually a thought behind it. So… no, I don’t think I get anxious for no apparent reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were in a completely relaxed environment with no responsibilities or tasks to complete?\n\nPatient: I think… probably not, or at least not in the same way. If it were truly completely relaxed, like nothing I had to do, nothing I was supposed to remember, no one depending on me to finish something or get something right, then I don’t think I’d just suddenly feel anxious for no reason.\n\nBut it’s a little hard for me to imagine, honestly, because even when things are supposed to be relaxed, my mind can still kind of bring things back in. So if I’m in a calm place but then I start thinking about whether I forgot something or whether there’s something I should be doing, then I might feel anxious. But if you mean really no responsibilities, no tasks, and I’m not thinking about any of that… then I don’t think I’d feel anxious. I’d probably just feel relieved, maybe a little unfamiliar with it, but not anxious in that intense way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were in a situation where you couldn't check or control things?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think that would probably make me really anxious, honestly. The not being able to check is kind of the worst part for me.\n\nLike with work, if I’m doing spreadsheets or calculations, I can’t just look at them once and move on. I’ll go back over the same numbers again and again, even when I know I already checked them. Sometimes I’ll tell myself, okay, that’s enough, you checked it, but then I get this feeling like… what if I missed something? What if there’s one mistake in there and it causes a problem and then it’s my fault? And it doesn’t feel like a normal “maybe I should review it” thing. It feels like I have to do it one more time or something bad is going to happen.\n\nSo if I were in a situation where I couldn’t check or control it, I think I’d feel trapped. I’d probably keep thinking about it over and over in my head, and I’d want to go back so badly. I’ve stayed at work for hours after everyone else has left because I couldn’t stop going back through things, even when part of me knew I had already checked them. It’s embarrassing to say, but not being able to do that would make me feel like I was leaving something dangerous unfinished.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would completely go away if you could somehow stop having these repetitive thoughts about checking and making mistakes?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, mostly. I mean, if those thoughts actually stopped, I think the anxiety would drop a lot, because it’s not like I’m just sitting there calmly and then my body panics out of nowhere. It’s more like the thought hits me first.\n\nEven after I know the numbers match, like I’ve already checked payroll or taxes and everything lines up, this thought keeps jumping in like, “What if there’s one hidden mistake you didn’t see?” And then I start imagining somebody’s check not going through, or the firm getting in trouble, or someone finding out later that I missed something obvious. And I try to tell myself, no, you looked at it, the numbers match, but it doesn’t really settle. It comes right back, like I can’t trust my own eyes.\n\nSo if I could somehow stop that loop from starting, or if I could believe the first check and move on, I think I would feel a lot less anxious. Maybe not perfectly relaxed, because I don’t know what that would even feel like at work anymore, but the really bad anxiety seems tied to that repeated thought that I missed something and need to check again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever feel anxious if you weren't having these thoughts about needing to check things?\n\nPatient: I think if I genuinely wasn’t having those thoughts, then no, not in that same way. The anxiety seems to come after the thought shows up, like it starts with, “What if you missed something?” and then everything in me reacts to that.\n\nBecause even when I know the numbers match — like I’ve checked the payroll or the taxes and I can see that it lines up — this thought still jumps in that there’s some hidden mistake I didn’t catch. Like maybe somebody’s check won’t go through, or the firm will get in trouble, or someone will find out later that I missed something obvious. And I try to push it away, I really do. I’ll tell myself, “You already checked this, it’s fine,” but then it comes right back almost immediately, like I can’t trust my own eyes.\n\nSo if that part wasn’t happening, if I wasn’t getting that repeated thought that I need to check because something could be wrong, I think I’d feel mostly okay. Maybe normal stress sometimes, but not that intense anxious feeling where I can’t move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever feel anxious if you weren't having these specific thoughts about work and checking things?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it wasn’t those exact thoughts about work and checking, I don’t think I’d feel that same kind of anxiety. It’s really tied to that specific loop for me.\n\nLike, I can be looking right at the numbers, and they match. I know they match. I’ll have checked payroll or taxes, and on paper everything lines up, but then this thought just jumps in anyway, like, what if there’s one hidden mistake you didn’t catch? What if somebody’s check doesn’t go through because of you, or the firm gets in trouble, or someone finds out later that you missed something obvious?\n\nAnd I try to push it away. I’ll tell myself, you saw it, you checked it, it’s fine. But it comes right back, and then I start feeling like I can’t trust what I just looked at with my own eyes. So if those specific thoughts weren’t there, I think I’d probably just have normal work stress, maybe, but not that awful anxious feeling where I feel like I have to keep going back.\n\nDoctor: Would you ever feel anxious if you weren't having any obsessive thoughts at all?\n\nPatient: I guess if I truly wasn’t having any of those thoughts at all, then I don’t think I’d have that same anxiety. Or at least not the kind that takes over.\n\nFor me it’s like the anxiety is attached to the thought. I’ll already know the numbers match — like with payroll or taxes, I can literally see that it adds up — and then this other thought jumps in anyway, like, “What if there’s one hidden mistake you missed?” And then it turns into, “What if someone’s check doesn’t go through?” or “What if the firm gets in trouble because of you?” And even when I try to push it away and tell myself I already checked it, it just comes right back.\n\nSo if those thoughts weren’t there at all… I think I’d probably feel mostly okay. Maybe I’d still get normal stress like anyone would, but not that awful feeling where I can’t trust my own eyes and I feel pulled back into checking again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever feel anxious without having any obsessive thoughts?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I would, not in that same way. I mean, I might get normal stress, like anybody would, but the really intense anxiety seems to come from that thought jumping in.\n\nIt’s like even after I know the numbers match — I can be looking right at payroll or taxes and see that it lines up — there’s still this thought that maybe I missed one hidden mistake. And then it kind of spirals from there, like maybe somebody’s check won’t go through, or maybe the firm will get in trouble, and it’ll somehow be because I didn’t catch something obvious.\n\nAnd I try to push it away. I really do. I’ll tell myself, “You already checked it, you saw it with your own eyes,” but it comes right back anyway. That’s the part that makes me anxious, like I suddenly can’t trust what I just saw. So without that kind of thought, I don’t think I’d feel that same panic or pressure to keep checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever feel anxious without any thoughts that you consider obsessive or intrusive?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, honestly. Not that kind of anxiety. I mean, I can get stressed like anyone can, but the anxiety that really grabs me seems to start when that thought pops in.\n\nIt’s usually after I’ve already checked something, like payroll or taxes, and I know the numbers match. I can be looking right at it and it adds up, and for a second I feel like, okay, this is fine. But then this thought jumps in like, what if there’s one hidden mistake you missed? What if someone’s check doesn’t go through, or what if the firm gets in trouble because you didn’t catch it?\n\nAnd I try to push it away. I’ll tell myself, no, you saw it, you checked it, it matched. But it comes back anyway, and then I start feeling like I can’t trust my own eyes, like what I just looked at somehow doesn’t count unless I go back again. So if I wasn’t having thoughts like that, the ones that just force their way in, I don’t think I’d feel that same anxious feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever feel anxious without having any thoughts that you consider obsessive or intrusive?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I would, at least not that same kind of anxious feeling. It’s like the anxiety doesn’t just appear out of nowhere for me — it comes right after the thought, or maybe with the thought.\n\nLike, I can be doing payroll or taxes and I’ll know the numbers match. I mean, I’ll actually see it. I’ll check the totals, and it lines up, and part of me knows it’s okay. But then this thought jumps in anyway, like, “What if there’s one mistake you didn’t notice?” Not even a big obvious one, but some hidden mistake that I somehow missed.\n\nAnd then it goes from that to, “What if somebody’s check doesn’t go through?” or “What if the firm gets in trouble because of this?” And then I try to push it away, because I know I already checked it. I’ll tell myself, “You saw it, it matched, you don’t need to go back.” But it comes right back, and then I feel like I can’t trust my own eyes, like what I just saw doesn’t count anymore.\n\nSo without that kind of thought forcing its way in, I don’t think I’d be anxious in that same way. Maybe normal stress, sure, but not that pressure where I feel like I have to keep going back and checking.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F42.2", "F41.1"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
If I truly wasn’t having any thoughts at all, I don’t think I’d feel that same anxiety. For me it’s tied so closely to the thought showing up.

Like with payroll or taxes, I can be sitting there looking at the numbers and they match. I know they match. I’ll have checked the totals, maybe even more than once, and for a second I can see that it’s fine. But then it’s like this little thought forces its way in anyway — what if there’s one hidden mistake you didn’t catch? What if somebody’s check doesn’t go through because of you? What if the firm gets in trouble and it all comes back to something you missed?

And I try to push it away because I know, logically, I already looked. I’ll tell myself, “You saw it. The numbers matched. You don’t need to go back.” But it doesn’t really stay gone. It comes right back, and then I start feeling like I can’t trust what I just saw with my own eyes.

So if there were no thoughts at all, no “what if I missed something” thought, then no, I don’t think that panic would really be there. It’s the thought that starts it.

I don’t think I usually feel anxious if I’m just… not thinking about work or not in that checking mode. At least, I haven’t noticed that happening. I might feel tense sometimes, but when I stop and look at it, there’s usually a thought behind it. If it were truly completely relaxed, like nothing I had to do, nothing I was supposed to remember, no one depending on me to finish something or get something right, then I don’t think I’d just suddenly feel anxious for no reason.\n\nBut it’s a little hard for me to imagine, honestly, because even when things are supposed to be relaxed, my mind can still kind of bring things back in. So if I’m in a calm place but then I start thinking about whether I forgot something or whether there’s something I should be doing, then I might feel anxious. But if you mean really no responsibilities, no tasks, and I’m not thinking about any of that… then I don’t think I’d feel anxious. I’d probably just feel relieved, maybe a little unfamiliar with it, but not anxious in that intense way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were in a situation where you couldn't check or control things?\n\nPatient: Yes. The not being able to check is kind of the worst part for me.\n\nLike with work, if I’m doing spreadsheets or calculations, I can’t just look at them once and move on. I’ll go back over the same numbers again and again, even when I know I already checked them. Sometimes I’ll tell myself, okay, that’s enough, you checked it, but then I get this feeling like… what if I missed something? What if there’s one mistake in there and it causes a problem and then it’s my fault? And it doesn’t feel like a normal “maybe I should review it” thing. It feels like I have to do it one more time or something bad is going to happen.\n\nSo if I were in a situation where I couldn’t check or control it, I think I’d feel trapped. I’d probably keep thinking about it over and over in my head, and I’d want to go back so badly. I’ve stayed at work for hours after everyone else has left because I couldn’t stop going back through things, even when part of me knew I had already checked them. It’s embarrassing to say, but not being able to do that would make me feel like I was leaving something dangerous unfinished.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would completely go away if you could somehow stop having these repetitive thoughts about checking and making mistakes?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, mostly. Maybe not perfectly relaxed, because I don’t know what that would even feel like at work anymore, but the really bad anxiety seems tied to that repeated thought that I missed something and need to check again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever feel anxious if you weren't having these thoughts about needing to check things?\n\nPatient: I think if I genuinely wasn’t having those thoughts, then no, not in that same way. The anxiety seems to come after the thought shows up, like it starts with, “What if you missed something?” and then everything in me reacts to that.\n\nBecause even when I know the numbers match — like I’ve checked the payroll or the taxes and I can see that it lines up — this thought still jumps in that there’s some hidden mistake I didn’t catch. Like maybe somebody’s check won’t go through, or the firm will get in trouble, or someone will find out later that I missed something obvious. And I try to push it away, I really do. I’ll tell myself, “You already checked this, it’s fine,” but then it comes right back almost immediately, like I can’t trust my own eyes.\n\nSo if that part wasn’t happening, if I wasn’t getting that repeated thought that I need to check because something could be wrong, I think I’d feel mostly okay. Maybe normal stress sometimes, but not that intense anxious feeling where I can’t move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever feel anxious if you weren't having these specific thoughts about work and checking things?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it wasn’t those exact thoughts about work and checking, I don’t think I’d feel that same kind of anxiety. I’ll have checked payroll or taxes, and on paper everything lines up, but then this thought just jumps in anyway, like, what if there’s one hidden mistake you didn’t catch? What if somebody’s check doesn’t go through because of you, or the firm gets in trouble, or someone finds out later that you missed something obvious?\n\nAnd I try to push it away. I’ll already know the numbers match — like with payroll or taxes, I can literally see that it adds up — and then this other thought jumps in anyway, like, “What if there’s one hidden mistake you missed?” And then it turns into, “What if someone’s check doesn’t go through?” or “What if the firm gets in trouble because of you?” And even when I try to push it away and tell myself I already checked it, it just comes right back.\n\nSo if those thoughts weren’t there at all… I think I’d probably feel mostly okay. Maybe I’d still get normal stress like anyone would, but not that awful feeling where I can’t trust my own eyes and I feel pulled back into checking again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever feel anxious without having any obsessive thoughts?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I would, not in that same way. I mean, I might get normal stress, like anybody would, but the really intense anxiety seems to come from that thought jumping in.\n\nIt’s like even after I know the numbers match — I can be looking right at payroll or taxes and see that it lines up — there’s still this thought that maybe I missed one hidden mistake. And then it kind of spirals from there, like maybe somebody’s check won’t go through, or maybe the firm will get in trouble, and it’ll somehow be because I didn’t catch something obvious.\n\nAnd I try to push it away. I really do. I’ll tell myself, “You already checked it, you saw it with your own eyes,” but it comes right back anyway. That’s the part that makes me anxious, like I suddenly can’t trust what I just saw. So without that kind of thought, I don’t think I’d feel that same panic or pressure to keep checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever feel anxious without any thoughts that you consider obsessive or intrusive?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, honestly. Not that kind of anxiety. I mean, I can get stressed like anyone can, but the anxiety that really grabs me seems to start when that thought pops in.\n\nIt’s usually after I’ve already checked something, like payroll or taxes, and I know the numbers match. I can be looking right at it and it adds up, and for a second I feel like, okay, this is fine. But then this thought jumps in like, what if there’s one hidden mistake you missed? What if someone’s check doesn’t go through, or what if the firm gets in trouble because you didn’t catch it?\n\nAnd I try to push it away. I’ll tell myself, no, you saw it, you checked it, it matched. But it comes back anyway, and then I start feeling like I can’t trust my own eyes, like what I just looked at somehow doesn’t count unless I go back again. So if I wasn’t having thoughts like that, the ones that just force their way in, I don’t think I’d feel that same anxious feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever feel anxious without having any thoughts that you consider obsessive or intrusive?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I would, at least not that same kind of anxious feeling. It’s like the anxiety doesn’t just appear out of nowhere for me — it comes right after the thought, or maybe with the thought.\n\nLike, I can be doing payroll or taxes and I’ll know the numbers match. I mean, I’ll actually see it. I’ll check the totals, and it lines up, and part of me knows it’s okay. But then this thought jumps in anyway, like, “What if there’s one mistake you didn’t notice?” Not even a big obvious one, but some hidden mistake that I somehow missed.\n\nAnd then it goes from that to, “What if somebody’s check doesn’t go through?” or “What if the firm gets in trouble because of this?” And then I try to push it away, because I know I already checked it. I’ll tell myself, “You saw it, it matched, you don’t need to go back.” But it comes right back, and then I feel like I can’t trust my own eyes, like what I just saw doesn’t count anymore.\n\nSo without that kind of thought forcing its way in, I don’t think I’d be anxious in that same way. Maybe normal stress, sure, but not that pressure where I feel like I have to keep going back and checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you weren't having any thoughts at all?\n\nPatient: If I truly wasn’t having any thoughts at all, I don’t think I’d feel that same anxiety. For me it’s tied so closely to the thought showing up.\n\nLike with payroll or taxes, I can be sitting there looking at the numbers and they match. I know they match. I’ll have checked the totals, maybe even more than once, and for a second I can see that it’s fine. But then it’s like this little thought forces its way in anyway — what if there’s one hidden mistake you didn’t catch? What if somebody’s check doesn’t go through because of you? So if I’m in a calm place but then I start thinking about whether I forgot something or whether there’s something I should be doing, then I might feel anxious. Sometimes I’ll tell myself, okay, that’s enough, you checked it, but then I get this feeling like… what if I missed something? It’s embarrassing to say, but not being able to do that would make me feel like I was leaving something dangerous unfinished.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would completely go away if you could somehow stop having these repetitive thoughts about checking and making mistakes?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, mostly. So if I wasn’t having thoughts like that, the ones that just force their way in, I don’t think I’d feel that same anxious feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever feel anxious without having any thoughts that you consider obsessive or intrusive?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I would, at least not that same kind of anxious feeling. It’s like the anxiety doesn’t just appear out of nowhere for me — it comes right after the thought, or maybe with the thought.\n\nLike, I can be doing payroll or taxes and I’ll know the numbers match. I mean, I’ll actually see it. I’ll check the totals, and it lines up, and part of me knows it’s okay. But then this thought jumps in anyway, like, “What if there’s one mistake you didn’t notice?” Not even a big obvious one, but some hidden mistake that I somehow missed.\n\nAnd then it goes from that to, “What if somebody’s check doesn’t go through?” or “What if the firm gets in trouble because of this?” And then I try to push it away, because I know I already checked it. I’ll tell myself, “You saw it, it matched, you don’t need to go back.” But it comes right back, and then I feel like I can’t trust my own eyes, like what I just saw doesn’t count anymore.\n\nSo without that kind of thought forcing its way in, I don’t think I’d be anxious in that same way. Maybe normal stress, sure, but not that pressure where I feel like I have to keep going back and checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you weren't having any thoughts at all?\n\nPatient: If I truly wasn’t having any thoughts at all, I don’t think I’d feel that same anxiety. For me it’s tied so closely to the thought showing up.\n\nLike with payroll or taxes, I can be sitting there looking at the numbers and they match. I know they match. I’ll have checked the totals, maybe even more than once, and for a second I can see that it’s fine. But then it’s like this little thought forces its way in anyway — what if there’s one hidden mistake you didn’t catch? What if somebody’s check doesn’t go through because of you? What if the firm gets in trouble and it all comes back to something you missed?\n\nAnd I try to push it away because I know, logically, I already looked. I’ll tell myself, “You saw it. The numbers matched. You don’t need to go back.” But it doesn’t really stay gone. It comes right back, and then I start feeling like I can’t trust what I just saw with my own eyes.\n\nSo if there were no thoughts at all, no “what if I missed something” thought, then no, I don’t think that panic would really be there. It’s the thought that starts it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would ever feel anxious without any obsessive thoughts at all?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 23 / bookkeeping_accounting_or_auditing_clerk / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Obsessions\nmanifestation: \"Even after I know the numbers match, this thought keeps jumping in that I missed one hidden mistake in payroll or taxes and somebody's check won't go through or the firm will get in trouble, and I try to push it away but it comes right back like I can't trust my own eyes.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t really think I would, not in the way I mean when I say I’m anxious. It’s like it always has a starting point, and the starting point is that thought.

Like I’ll already have checked the payroll or the tax numbers, and I can see they match. I know I’m not just guessing — I’m looking right at the totals, and they line up. But then this thought still jumps in, like, what if there’s one hidden mistake in there that I didn’t catch? And then it immediately turns into, what if someone’s check doesn’t go through, or what if the firm gets in trouble because I missed something?

I try to push it away. I’ll tell myself, you checked it, the numbers matched, you saw it with your own eyes. But it comes right back, and then I start feeling like I can’t trust what I just saw. Like somehow my own checking doesn’t count anymore unless I go back and do it again.

So if that thought wasn’t there at all, I don’t think I’d feel that same anxious feeling. It’s really the thought that sets it off.

If it were truly completely relaxed, like nothing I had to do, nothing I was supposed to remember, no one depending on me to finish something or get something right, then I don’t think I’d just suddenly feel anxious for no reason.\n\nBut it’s a little hard for me to imagine, honestly, because even when things are supposed to be relaxed, my mind can still kind of bring things back in. So if I’m in a calm place but then I start thinking about whether I forgot something or whether there’s something I should be doing, then I might feel anxious. I’d probably just feel relieved, maybe a little unfamiliar with it, but not anxious in that intense way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were in a situation where you couldn't check or control things?\n\nPatient: Yes. What if there’s one mistake in there and it causes a problem and then it’s my fault? And it doesn’t feel like a normal “maybe I should review it” thing. It feels like I have to do it one more time or something bad is going to happen.\n\nSo if I were in a situation where I couldn’t check or control it, I think I’d feel trapped. I’ve stayed at work for hours after everyone else has left because I couldn’t stop going back through things, even when part of me knew I had already checked them. It’s embarrassing to say, but not being able to do that would make me feel like I was leaving something dangerous unfinished.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would completely go away if you could somehow stop having these repetitive thoughts about checking and making mistakes?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, mostly. And I try to tell myself, no, you looked at it, the numbers match, but it doesn’t really settle. The anxiety seems to come after the thought shows up, like it starts with, “What if you missed something?” and then everything in me reacts to that.\n\nBecause even when I know the numbers match — like I’ve checked the payroll or the taxes and I can see that it lines up — this thought still jumps in that there’s some hidden mistake I didn’t catch. Not that kind of anxiety. So… no, I don’t think I get anxious for no apparent reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were in a completely relaxed environment with no responsibilities or tasks to complete?\n\nPatient: I think… probably not, or at least not in the same way. If it were truly completely relaxed, like nothing I had to do, nothing I was supposed to remember, no one depending on me to finish something or get something right, then I don’t think I’d just suddenly feel anxious for no reason.\n\nBut it’s a little hard for me to imagine, honestly, because even when things are supposed to be relaxed, my mind can still kind of bring things back in. So if I’m in a calm place but then I start thinking about whether I forgot something or whether there’s something I should be doing, then I might feel anxious. But if you mean really no responsibilities, no tasks, and I’m not thinking about any of that… then I don’t think I’d feel anxious. I’d probably just feel relieved, maybe a little unfamiliar with it, but not anxious in that intense way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were in a situation where you couldn't check or control things?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think that would probably make me really anxious, honestly. The not being able to check is kind of the worst part for me.\n\nLike with work, if I’m doing spreadsheets or calculations, I can’t just look at them once and move on. I’ll go back over the same numbers again and again, even when I know I already checked them. Sometimes I’ll tell myself, okay, that’s enough, you checked it, but then I get this feeling like… what if I missed something? What if there’s one mistake in there and it causes a problem and then it’s my fault? And it doesn’t feel like a normal “maybe I should review it” thing. It feels like I have to do it one more time or something bad is going to happen.\n\nSo if I were in a situation where I couldn’t check or control it, I think I’d feel trapped. I’d probably keep thinking about it over and over in my head, and I’d want to go back so badly. I’ve stayed at work for hours after everyone else has left because I couldn’t stop going back through things, even when part of me knew I had already checked them. It’s embarrassing to say, but not being able to do that would make me feel like I was leaving something dangerous unfinished.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would completely go away if you could somehow stop having these repetitive thoughts about checking and making mistakes?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, mostly. I mean, if those thoughts actually stopped, I think the anxiety would drop a lot, because it’s not like I’m just sitting there calmly and then my body panics out of nowhere. It’s more like the thought hits me first.\n\nEven after I know the numbers match, like I’ve already checked payroll or taxes and everything lines up, this thought keeps jumping in like, “What if there’s one hidden mistake you didn’t see?” And then I start imagining somebody’s check not going through, or the firm getting in trouble, or someone finding out later that I missed something obvious. And I try to tell myself, no, you looked at it, the numbers match, but it doesn’t really settle. It comes right back, like I can’t trust my own eyes.\n\nSo if I could somehow stop that loop from starting, or if I could believe the first check and move on, I think I would feel a lot less anxious. Maybe not perfectly relaxed, because I don’t know what that would even feel like at work anymore, but the really bad anxiety seems tied to that repeated thought that I missed something and need to check again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever feel anxious if you weren't having these thoughts about needing to check things?\n\nPatient: I think if I genuinely wasn’t having those thoughts, then no, not in that same way. The anxiety seems to come after the thought shows up, like it starts with, “What if you missed something?” and then everything in me reacts to that.\n\nBecause even when I know the numbers match — like I’ve checked the payroll or the taxes and I can see that it lines up — this thought still jumps in that there’s some hidden mistake I didn’t catch. Like maybe somebody’s check won’t go through, or the firm will get in trouble, or someone will find out later that I missed something obvious. And I try to push it away, I really do. I’ll tell myself, “You already checked this, it’s fine,” but then it comes right back almost immediately, like I can’t trust my own eyes.\n\nSo if that part wasn’t happening, if I wasn’t getting that repeated thought that I need to check because something could be wrong, I think I’d feel mostly okay. Maybe normal stress sometimes, but not that intense anxious feeling where I can’t move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever feel anxious if you weren't having these specific thoughts about work and checking things?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it wasn’t those exact thoughts about work and checking, I don’t think I’d feel that same kind of anxiety. It’s really tied to that specific loop for me.\n\nLike, I can be looking right at the numbers, and they match. I know they match. I’ll have checked payroll or taxes, and on paper everything lines up, but then this thought just jumps in anyway, like, what if there’s one hidden mistake you didn’t catch? What if somebody’s check doesn’t go through because of you, or the firm gets in trouble, or someone finds out later that you missed something obvious?\n\nAnd I try to push it away. I’ll tell myself, you saw it, you checked it, it’s fine. But it comes right back, and then I start feeling like I can’t trust what I just looked at with my own eyes. So if those specific thoughts weren’t there, I think I’d probably just have normal work stress, maybe, but not that awful anxious feeling where I feel like I have to keep going back.\n\nDoctor: Would you ever feel anxious if you weren't having any obsessive thoughts at all?\n\nPatient: I guess if I truly wasn’t having any of those thoughts at all, then I don’t think I’d have that same anxiety. Or at least not the kind that takes over.\n\nFor me it’s like the anxiety is attached to the thought. I’ll already know the numbers match — like with payroll or taxes, I can literally see that it adds up — and then this other thought jumps in anyway, like, “What if there’s one hidden mistake you missed?” And then it turns into, “What if someone’s check doesn’t go through?” or “What if the firm gets in trouble because of you?” And even when I try to push it away and tell myself I already checked it, it just comes right back.\n\nSo if those thoughts weren’t there at all… I think I’d probably feel mostly okay. Maybe I’d still get normal stress like anyone would, but not that awful feeling where I can’t trust my own eyes and I feel pulled back into checking again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever feel anxious without having any obsessive thoughts?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I would, not in that same way. I mean, I might get normal stress, like anybody would, but the really intense anxiety seems to come from that thought jumping in.\n\nIt’s like even after I know the numbers match — I can be looking right at payroll or taxes and see that it lines up — there’s still this thought that maybe I missed one hidden mistake. And then it kind of spirals from there, like maybe somebody’s check won’t go through, or maybe the firm will get in trouble, and it’ll somehow be because I didn’t catch something obvious.\n\nAnd I try to push it away. I really do. I’ll tell myself, “You already checked it, you saw it with your own eyes,” but it comes right back anyway. That’s the part that makes me anxious, like I suddenly can’t trust what I just saw. So without that kind of thought, I don’t think I’d feel that same panic or pressure to keep checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever feel anxious without any thoughts that you consider obsessive or intrusive?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, honestly. Not that kind of anxiety. I mean, I can get stressed like anyone can, but the anxiety that really grabs me seems to start when that thought pops in.\n\nIt’s usually after I’ve already checked something, like payroll or taxes, and I know the numbers match. I can be looking right at it and it adds up, and for a second I feel like, okay, this is fine. But then this thought jumps in like, what if there’s one hidden mistake you missed? What if someone’s check doesn’t go through, or what if the firm gets in trouble because you didn’t catch it?\n\nAnd I try to push it away. I’ll tell myself, no, you saw it, you checked it, it matched. But it comes back anyway, and then I start feeling like I can’t trust my own eyes, like what I just looked at somehow doesn’t count unless I go back again. So if I wasn’t having thoughts like that, the ones that just force their way in, I don’t think I’d feel that same anxious feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever feel anxious without having any thoughts that you consider obsessive or intrusive?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I would, at least not that same kind of anxious feeling. It’s like the anxiety doesn’t just appear out of nowhere for me — it comes right after the thought, or maybe with the thought.\n\nLike, I can be doing payroll or taxes and I’ll know the numbers match. I mean, I’ll actually see it. I’ll check the totals, and it lines up, and part of me knows it’s okay. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really. I mean, I’m sure I can feel normal stress sometimes, like anyone would, but the kind of anxiety I’m talking about always seems to come with that thought first.

It’s like I’ll be looking at payroll or tax numbers, and I know they match. I can see the totals line up, and I’ll think, okay, this is right. But then almost immediately there’s this thought that jumps in, like, what if there’s one hidden mistake I didn’t catch? Not something obvious, but something small buried in there that I somehow missed. And then it starts turning into, what if somebody’s check doesn’t go through, or what if the firm gets in trouble because I didn’t catch it?

I try to push it away because I know I already looked. I’ll tell myself I saw it with my own eyes, the numbers matched, I don’t need to go back. But it comes right back anyway, and then I start feeling like I can’t trust what I just saw.

So if that thought isn’t there, I don’t think the anxiety is really there in the same way. It’s like the thought is what lights it up.

Like some hidden error, some tiny thing I missed, and then someone’s paycheck won’t go through, or the firm will get in trouble, and it’ll be because I didn’t catch it.\n\nAnd I try to tell myself, “No, I literally just checked that,” but it doesn’t stick. It’s like I can’t trust what I just saw with my own eyes. The thought comes right back, almost immediately sometimes, like, “Are you sure? Are you really sure?” And then I start feeling like if I ignore it, I’m being careless or irresponsible, even when part of me knows I’m not. It just keeps repeating until I go back and look again.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and behaviors affecting your relationships with family, friends, or colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yes… it is, more than I wanted to admit at first. At work it’s gotten really bad. I actually missed a filing deadline because I couldn’t stop going back over everything, and then I got called into a formal meeting about it. That was horrible, because I was trying so hard to make sure nothing was wrong, and then the checking itself made me miss the actual deadline.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’m just not showing up for things anymore. I used to go to choir rehearsals, and I had commitments at the youth center, and lately I keep missing them because I can’t get out of the office on time. I’ll think I’m leaving at a normal hour, and then I tell myself I just need to check one more section, and suddenly it’s too late. So people are depending on me and I’m not there, and I feel really guilty and embarrassed about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed with anxiety in situations that have nothing to do with your work or checking things?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not completely sure. I don’t think I feel that same kind of anxiety in random situations, if that’s what you mean. Like, I don’t walk around feeling overwhelmed all the time for no reason.\n\nIt’s more that I get overwhelmed when something feels like it’s on me to get right, or when I start worrying I might have missed something. Outside of that… I mean, I can feel stressed like anyone does, but I haven’t really noticed the same intense feeling unless it’s tied to checking or being responsible for something. I guess I’m not sure if I’m answering that the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about work or checking things?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I usually feel anxious if I’m just… not thinking about work or not in that checking mode. Like if I’m just watching something, or talking to someone, or doing something ordinary, I’m not necessarily panicking or feeling that same pressure.\n\nBut I guess it’s hard to separate sometimes, because work kind of follows me in my head. So I might be technically not at work, but then if the thought pops up again, it’s like I’m back in it. If it truly has nothing to do with checking or whether I missed something, then… no, I don’t think the anxiety is there in the same way. At least I haven’t noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious when you are not thinking about work or checking things and there is no apparent reason for the anxiety?\n\nPatient: I… don’t think so, not really. I’m trying to think of a time when I felt anxious and there wasn’t some reason attached to it, and I can’t really come up with one. It’s usually connected to something in my head, like worrying I forgot something or that I need to go back and make sure.\n\nIf I’m genuinely not thinking about work or checking or whether I made a mistake, then I don’t usually feel that same anxiety just out of nowhere. At least, I haven’t noticed that happening. I might feel tense sometimes, but when I stop and look at it, there’s usually a thought behind it. So… no, I don’t think I get anxious for no apparent reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were in a completely relaxed environment with no responsibilities or tasks to complete?\n\nPatient: I think… probably not, or at least not in the same way. If it were truly completely relaxed, like nothing I had to do, nothing I was supposed to remember, no one depending on me to finish something or get something right, then I don’t think I’d just suddenly feel anxious for no reason.\n\nBut it’s a little hard for me to imagine, honestly, because even when things are supposed to be relaxed, my mind can still kind of bring things back in. So if I’m in a calm place but then I start thinking about whether I forgot something or whether there’s something I should be doing, then I might feel anxious. But if you mean really no responsibilities, no tasks, and I’m not thinking about any of that… then I don’t think I’d feel anxious. I’d probably just feel relieved, maybe a little unfamiliar with it, but not anxious in that intense way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were in a situation where you couldn't check or control things?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think that would probably make me really anxious, honestly. The not being able to check is kind of the worst part for me.\n\nLike with work, if I’m doing spreadsheets or calculations, I can’t just look at them once and move on. I’ll go back over the same numbers again and again, even when I know I already checked them. Sometimes I’ll tell myself, okay, that’s enough, you checked it, but then I get this feeling like… what if I missed something? What if there’s one mistake in there and it causes a problem and then it’s my fault? And it doesn’t feel like a normal “maybe I should review it” thing. It feels like I have to do it one more time or something bad is going to happen.\n\nSo if I were in a situation where I couldn’t check or control it, I think I’d feel trapped. I’d probably keep thinking about it over and over in my head, and I’d want to go back so badly. I’ve stayed at work for hours after everyone else has left because I couldn’t stop going back through things, even when part of me knew I had already checked them. It’s embarrassing to say, but not being able to do that would make me feel like I was leaving something dangerous unfinished.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would completely go away if you could somehow stop having these repetitive thoughts about checking and making mistakes?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, mostly. I mean, if those thoughts actually stopped, I think the anxiety would drop a lot, because it’s not like I’m just sitting there calmly and then my body panics out of nowhere. It’s more like the thought hits me first.\n\nEven after I know the numbers match, like I’ve already checked payroll or taxes and everything lines up, this thought keeps jumping in like, “What if there’s one hidden mistake you didn’t see?” And then I start imagining somebody’s check not going through, or the firm getting in trouble, or someone finding out later that I missed something obvious. And I try to tell myself, no, you looked at it, the numbers match, but it doesn’t really settle. It comes right back, like I can’t trust my own eyes.\n\nSo if I could somehow stop that loop from starting, or if I could believe the first check and move on, I think I would feel a lot less anxious. Maybe not perfectly relaxed, because I don’t know what that would even feel like at work anymore, but the really bad anxiety seems tied to that repeated thought that I missed something and need to check again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever feel anxious if you weren't having these thoughts about needing to check things?\n\nPatient: I think if I genuinely wasn’t having those thoughts, then no, not in that same way. The anxiety seems to come after the thought shows up, like it starts with, “What if you missed something?” and then everything in me reacts to that.\n\nBecause even when I know the numbers match — like I’ve checked the payroll or the taxes and I can see that it lines up — this thought still jumps in that there’s some hidden mistake I didn’t catch. Like maybe somebody’s check won’t go through, or the firm will get in trouble, or someone will find out later that I missed something obvious. And I try to push it away, I really do. I’ll tell myself, “You already checked this, it’s fine,” but then it comes right back almost immediately, like I can’t trust my own eyes.\n\nSo if that part wasn’t happening, if I wasn’t getting that repeated thought that I need to check because something could be wrong, I think I’d feel mostly okay. Maybe normal stress sometimes, but not that intense anxious feeling where I can’t move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever feel anxious if you weren't having these specific thoughts about work and checking things?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it wasn’t those exact thoughts about work and checking, I don’t think I’d feel that same kind of anxiety. It’s really tied to that specific loop for me.\n\nLike, I can be looking right at the numbers, and they match. I know they match. I’ll have checked payroll or taxes, and on paper everything lines up, but then this thought just jumps in anyway, like, what if there’s one hidden mistake you didn’t catch? What if somebody’s check doesn’t go through because of you, or the firm gets in trouble, or someone finds out later that you missed something obvious?\n\nAnd I try to push it away. I’ll tell myself, you saw it, you checked it, it’s fine. But it comes right back, and then I start feeling like I can’t trust what I just looked at with my own eyes. So if those specific thoughts weren’t there, I think I’d probably just have normal work stress, maybe, but not that awful anxious feeling where I feel like I have to keep going back.\n\nDoctor: Would you ever feel anxious if you weren't having any obsessive thoughts at all?\n\nPatient: I guess if I truly wasn’t having any of those thoughts at all, then I don’t think I’d have that same anxiety. Or at least not the kind that takes over.\n\nFor me it’s like the anxiety is attached to the thought. I’ll already know the numbers match — like with payroll or taxes, I can literally see that it adds up — and then this other thought jumps in anyway, like, “What if there’s one hidden mistake you missed?” And then it turns into, “What if someone’s check doesn’t go through?” or “What if the firm gets in trouble because of you?” And even when I try to push it away and tell myself I already checked it, it just comes right back.\n\nSo if those thoughts weren’t there at all… I think I’d probably feel mostly okay. Maybe I’d still get normal stress like anyone would, but not that awful feeling where I can’t trust my own eyes and I feel pulled back into checking again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever feel anxious without having any obsessive thoughts?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I would, not in that same way. I mean, I might get normal stress, like anybody would, but the really intense anxiety seems to come from that thought jumping in.\n\nIt’s like even after I know the numbers match — I can be looking right at payroll or taxes and see that it lines up — there’s still this thought that maybe I missed one hidden mistake. And then it kind of spirals from there, like maybe somebody’s check won’t go through, or maybe the firm will get in trouble, and it’ll somehow be because I didn’t catch something obvious.\n\nAnd I try to push it away. I really do. I’ll tell myself, “You already checked it, you saw it with your own eyes,” but it comes right back anyway. That’s the part that makes me anxious, like I suddenly can’t trust what I just saw. So without that kind of thought, I don’t think I’d feel that same panic or pressure to keep checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever feel anxious without any thoughts that you consider obsessive or intrusive?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, honestly. Not that kind of anxiety. I mean, I can get stressed like anyone can, but the anxiety that really grabs me seems to start when that thought pops in.\n\nIt’s usually after I’ve already checked something, like payroll or taxes, and I know the numbers match. I can be looking right at it and it adds up, and for a second I feel like, okay, this is fine. But then this thought jumps in like, what if there’s one hidden mistake you missed? What if someone’s check doesn’t go through, or what if the firm gets in trouble because you didn’t catch it?\n\nAnd I try to push it away. I’ll tell myself, no, you saw it, you checked it, it matched. But it comes back anyway, and then I start feeling like I can’t trust my own eyes, like what I just looked at somehow doesn’t count unless I go back again. So if I wasn’t having thoughts like that, the ones that just force their way in, I don’t think I’d feel that same anxious feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever feel anxious without having any thoughts that you consider obsessive or intrusive?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I would, at least not that same kind of anxious feeling. It’s like the anxiety doesn’t just appear out of nowhere for me — it comes right after the thought, or maybe with the thought.\n\nLike, I can be doing payroll or taxes and I’ll know the numbers match. I mean, I’ll actually see it. I’ll check the totals, and it lines up, and part of me knows it’s okay. But then this thought jumps in anyway, like, “What if there’s one mistake you didn’t notice?” Not even a big obvious one, but some hidden mistake that I somehow missed.\n\nAnd then it goes from that to, “What if somebody’s check doesn’t go through?” or “What if the firm gets in trouble because of this?” And then I try to push it away, because I know I already checked it. I’ll tell myself, “You saw it, it matched, you don’t need to go back.” But it comes right back, and then I feel like I can’t trust my own eyes, like what I just saw doesn’t count anymore.\n\nSo without that kind of thought forcing its way in, I don’t think I’d be anxious in that same way. Maybe normal stress, sure, but not that pressure where I feel like I have to keep going back and checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you weren't having any thoughts at all?\n\nPatient: If I truly wasn’t having any thoughts at all, I don’t think I’d feel that same anxiety. For me it’s tied so closely to the thought showing up.\n\nLike with payroll or taxes, I can be sitting there looking at the numbers and they match. I know they match. I’ll have checked the totals, maybe even more than once, and for a second I can see that it’s fine. But then it’s like this little thought forces its way in anyway — what if there’s one hidden mistake you didn’t catch? What if somebody’s check doesn’t go through because of you? What if the firm gets in trouble and it all comes back to something you missed?\n\nAnd I try to push it away because I know, logically, I already looked. I’ll tell myself, “You saw it. The numbers matched. You don’t need to go back.” But it doesn’t really stay gone. It comes right back, and then I start feeling like I can’t trust what I just saw with my own eyes.\n\nSo if there were no thoughts at all, no “what if I missed something” thought, then no, I don’t think that panic would really be there. It’s the thought that starts it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever feel anxious without any obsessive thoughts at all?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t really think I would, not in the way I mean when I say I’m anxious. It’s like it always has a starting point, and the starting point is that thought.\n\nLike I’ll already have checked the payroll or the tax numbers, and I can see they match. I know I’m not just guessing — I’m looking right at the totals, and they line up. But then this thought still jumps in, like, what if there’s one hidden mistake in there that I didn’t catch? And then it immediately turns into, what if someone’s check doesn’t go through, or what if the firm gets in trouble because I missed something?\n\nI try to push it away. I’ll tell myself, you checked it, the numbers matched, you saw it with your own eyes. But it comes right back, and then I start feeling like I can’t trust what I just saw. Like somehow my own checking doesn’t count anymore unless I go back and do it again.\n\nSo if that thought wasn’t there at all, I don’t think I’d feel that same anxious feeling. It’s really the thought that sets it off.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you feel anxious without any obsessive thoughts being present?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I’m sure I can feel normal stress sometimes, like anyone would, but the kind of anxiety I’m talking about always seems to come with that thought first.\n\nIt’s like I’ll be looking at payroll or tax numbers, and I know they match. I can see the totals line up, and I’ll think, okay, this is right. But then almost immediately there’s this thought that jumps in, like, what if there’s one hidden mistake I didn’t catch? Not something obvious, but something small buried in there that I somehow missed. And then it starts turning into, what if somebody’s check doesn’t go through, or what if the firm gets in trouble because I didn’t catch it?\n\nI try to push it away because I know I already looked. I’ll tell myself I saw it with my own eyes, the numbers matched, I don’t need to go back. But it comes right back anyway, and then I start feeling like I can’t trust what I just saw.\n\nSo if that thought isn’t there, I don’t think the anxiety is really there in the same way. It’s like the thought is what lights it up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Or at least not the kind that takes over.\n\nFor me it’s like the anxiety is attached to the thought. I’ll already know the numbers match — like with payroll or taxes, I can literally see that it adds up — and then this other thought jumps in anyway, like, “What if there’s one hidden mistake you missed?” And then it turns into, “What if someone’s check doesn’t go through?” or “What if the firm gets in trouble because of you?” And even when I try to push it away and tell myself I already checked it, it just comes right back.\n\nSo if those thoughts weren’t there at all… I think I’d probably feel mostly okay. Maybe I’d still get normal stress like anyone would, but not that awful feeling where I can’t trust my own eyes and I feel pulled back into checking again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever feel anxious without having any obsessive thoughts?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I would, not in that same way. I mean, I might get normal stress, like anybody would, but the really intense anxiety seems to come from that thought jumping in.\n\nIt’s like even after I know the numbers match — I can be looking right at payroll or taxes and see that it lines up — there’s still this thought that maybe I missed one hidden mistake. And then it kind of spirals from there, like maybe somebody’s check won’t go through, or maybe the firm will get in trouble, and it’ll somehow be because I didn’t catch something obvious.\n\nAnd I try to push it away. That’s the part that makes me anxious, like I suddenly can’t trust what I just saw. So without that kind of thought, I don’t think I’d feel that same panic or pressure to keep checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever feel anxious without any thoughts that you consider obsessive or intrusive?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, honestly. Not that kind of anxiety. I mean, I can get stressed like anyone can, but the anxiety that really grabs me seems to start when that thought pops in.\n\nIt’s usually after I’ve already checked something, like payroll or taxes, and I know the numbers match. I can be looking right at it and it adds up, and for a second I feel like, okay, this is fine. But then this thought jumps in like, what if there’s one hidden mistake you missed? So if I wasn’t having thoughts like that, the ones that just force their way in, I don’t think I’d feel that same anxious feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever feel anxious without having any thoughts that you consider obsessive or intrusive?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I would, at least not that same kind of anxious feeling. It’s like the anxiety doesn’t just appear out of nowhere for me — it comes right after the thought, or maybe with the thought.\n\nLike, I can be doing payroll or taxes and I’ll know the numbers match. Maybe normal stress, sure, but not that pressure where I feel like I have to keep going back and checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you weren't having any thoughts at all?\n\nPatient: If I truly wasn’t having any thoughts at all, I don’t think I’d feel that same anxiety. I’ll have checked the totals, maybe even more than once, and for a second I can see that it’s fine. But then it’s like this little thought forces its way in anyway — what if there’s one hidden mistake you didn’t catch? What if somebody’s check doesn’t go through because of you? What if the firm gets in trouble and it all comes back to something you missed?\n\nAnd I try to push it away because I know, logically, I already looked. I’ll tell myself, “You saw it. It’s the thought that starts it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would ever feel anxious without any obsessive thoughts at all?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t really think I would, not in the way I mean when I say I’m anxious. It’s like it always has a starting point, and the starting point is that thought.\n\nLike I’ll already have checked the payroll or the tax numbers, and I can see they match.
